Katalog Berita (Halaman 45/57)

#2535. Aspect analysis: Leipzig never found a trigger – LC

von Next Generation am 20.10.2026 in den Kategorien Uncategorized mit 0 Kommentaren

A 6-1 reads like a game in which everything fell apart. Look at how the goals came about one by one and a different picture emerges: it was not six separate problems but two or three, which Bayern punished in seven variations. No pressure on the man in possession, marking problems in central areas, and a back line that did not work with the movements in front of it – that chain runs through practically every goal conceded.

This aspect analysis therefore takes the goals rather than the game as its material and works back from there to the patterns.

Bayern triggered very high throughout, stayed tight to their opponents and pushed through centrally without hesitation. Vandevoordt was pressed from Bayern’s right, with the clear intention of keeping him away from the left-hand build-up route and forcing the long ball. Leipzig’s front line of Nusa and Brajan Gruda repeatedly angled their runs deep against Nathaniel Brown and Konrad Laimer.

Bayern had one extra central player dropping into midfield in this phase, usually Kimmich. Had Leipzig played short to the right, he could have pressed through onto Raum; in practice he stayed central and pushed through from there. That left Leipzig with the long ball, and it was ineffective: it produced almost no regains, so the enforced forward play brought neither relief nor territory.

Going the other way, Leipzig got no access at all, and the reason was Bayern’s rotations. Kimmich dropped right, Pavlović left into a back three, with Upamecano holding a very wide position. Against this shifting three-man build-up Leipzig never found a trigger, because the assignments reset with every rotation – whoever was supposed to step out of midfield first had to work out who he was picking up. The handover of pressing duties out of central areas failed repeatedly.

In front of that, Bayern staggered their shape so that Leipzig’s compactness became the problem. Olise moved into the ten space at times, Nathaniel Brown operated in the half-lane and ran beyond from there, Luis Díaz stayed wide left. Saibari and Kane pinned several opponents centrally while regularly pulling out of the central lane. Because Leipzig pressed very narrow, this produced exactly what Bayern were looking for: once the opponent was committed near the ball, the switch followed, and on the now open side stood a one-on-one.

That already builds the bridge to the goals. The isolated one-on-ones in which Luis Díaz got his three finishes were not individual moments but the product of this sequence of pinning and switching.

1-0, Luis Díaz (12.) – The first goal already contains the full diagnosis. Kimmich receives centrally without any pressure and can turn out with an open body shape. Ahead of him Saibari and Kane occupy the central zone in front of the box, which forces Orban and Lukeba to step out – so far, the intended part. The full-backs, however, do not push up with that movement and stay too deep, while Raum’s position plays the runner onside. Klostermann stays outside and leaves the inside lane unsecured, and that is exactly where Luis Díaz starts from. Out of that unprotected lane Díaz runs into the back line and Kimmich chips the ball in behind. Not one bad decision, but four players whose decisions did not fit together.

2-0, Aleksandar Pavlović (26.) – Same cause, different route. Kane drops deep and Leipzig do not shift across as a unit – the reactions showed it clearly: arms thrown up, gesturing, hands dropping again, the beginnings of frustration. Kane gets the free body shape, and with him the consequence is predictable: the long ball, high or flat. Because Leipzig are so narrow centrally, the ball flies straight through the entire zone in front of their back line. Luis Díaz moves actively into the passing lane to control it while Klostermann tries to get into his running line and does not make it – Díaz reaches the ball first. From the one-on-one he wins, Díaz dribbles centrally at Orban, which creates a four-against-three on the break. Díaz pins Orban and Lukeba centrally, Pavlović overlaps on the left after holding a high, left-leaning position all evening and threatening the back line. Two touches, left foot, finished away from the defender.

3-0, Michael Olise (34.) – Here Bayern’s press shows itself at its clearest. From a Leipzig throw-in, the man-orientation is fully visible: consistent pushing through, clean handovers. A misplaced pass from Raum then cannot be defended, because neither the tracking nor the duelling is good enough to disturb the pass. Kane can open up half-way. Centrally, Olise comes inside from the wing, and Banzuzi and his partner fail to pick him up, letting him run through – against a player of that variability, an invitation to the key pass. Luis Díaz runs beyond and drags Orban with him, Klostermann is pinned wide by Saibari and can no longer get back into central areas. Olise finishes against the goalkeeper’s movement, across his standing leg into the far corner, catching Vandevoordt completely on the wrong foot.

4-0, Luis Díaz (47.) – After a regain, Bayern switch quickly to the far side and once diagonally through, putting Díaz in a one-on-one again. He holds the ball in the dribble, and Leipzig fail to solve the situation in three steps: the centre is not closed, Orban is too late stepping out, Klostermann does not get into the duel. That Vandevoordt leaves the far corner open is the final piece of an isolated one-on-one that should never have arisen.

5-0, Harry Kane (53.) – Structurally the most interesting goal. Leipzig play a long ball without preparing it and react to the consequences contradictorily: the back line does not push up at all, while the midfield line and both forwards counterpress forwards. The ten space opens up enormously, the gaps between the lines become huge – and Olise is standing in that zone. One pass from Kim is enough to take six Leipzig players out of the game. Olise runs unopposed at the back line and lays it off to Díaz, who is in a one-on-one again; Brown joins for the two-against-one and shifts into the inside lane. Klostermann does not pick up his run from the deep half-space, and centrally Kane again drops into Orban’s blind side – a pattern that stood out across the whole game. The result is a two-against-one against the goalkeeper.

6-0, Luis Díaz (69.) – From a set piece Bayern create a five-against-four out wide. Gnabry pins opponents and makes the space for Díaz, Orban again takes one big step in the block and does not get into the duel. The same scene as the 4-0, only from a different phase of play – a sign that this is a behavioural pattern rather than a snapshot.

6-1, Andrija Maksimović (90.+1) – Leipzig’s goal shows in reverse what their own game had been missing. Bayern drop into a deeper block here, and Leipzig combine centrally before going wide. Gruda gets into a one-on-one against Brown, Upamecano steps across – but from above, with too wide a gap and without closing the centre. That opens the one-two, which Leipzig take immediately. Because the whole situation narrows onto that combination, the space behind it stays unoccupied, and Maksimović can take two touches on his left and finish unopposed.

The sequence of goals reads like a repetition. Five of Bayern’s six come from the same combination of no pressure on the ball centrally and a back line that does not manage the space behind it. Luis Díaz stands in an isolated one-on-one three times, Orban is twice too late with a step that is too big, Kane repeatedly drops into his blind side. Klostermann certainly did not have his best day – but it would be too easy to settle the bill with him. In the decisive scenes he faced situations that functioning protection in front of him should never have allowed to arise in the first place.

Behind it all lies not an individual problem but a structural one: Leipzig pressed narrow and found no trigger against Bayern’s rotating back three. That very compactness made the switch of play the opponent’s most effective tool, and at the end of every switch stood a one-on-one that Leipzig lost. The scoreline is not the story – the story is that the same scene worked six times.

LC is often found in a classroom, happiest on a football pitch, and occasionally on LinkedIn.

#2534. 1. FC Köln vs Bayer Leverkusen preview: Local rivalry heats with Köln unbeaten at home

Köln are unbeaten in five home games this season with their last home game being the most impressive of all: a 5-0 win against Union Berlin in the DFB Pokal second round.

Alongside this, the hosts have improved their record massively defensively as they've conceded one goal in their last three games.

However, not all is bright as Köln has also only scored once in their last three games in somewhat of a drought in front of goal despite more shots (11) than Union Berlin in last weekend's 1-1 draw.

In spite of this, Malek El Mala was a bright shining light as he came on for his debut in Berlin against Union Berlin and scored within seven minutes to equalise with his younger brother Said involved in the goal.

Malek's brother, Said, has also rewarded himself with another Germany call-up for the games against Serbia and the Netherlands in the Nations League.

This goal holds even more weight as Köln are without strikers Imad Rondić and Thijs Dallinga with Ragnar Ache being doubtful for the weekend's tie.

Before the game against Leverkusen, Köln coach René Wagner said: “I hope we can create uncertainty right from the start. If we create an atmosphere, we have a great chance of winning the game.”

Currently Rav van den Berg, Thijs Dallinga, Ellyes Skhiri, Timo Hübers and Imad Rondić are all out injured.

The aforementioned Ragnar Ache remains a doubt and probably will only be fit enough to be on the bench.

Compounding a poor run of form recently, Bayer Leverkusen lost their first Europa League game against Marseille at home 1-0 in midweek.

For Leverkusen, the last two Bundesliga games have been rough as they've lost to Stuttgart and Hoffenheim by conceding three or more goals in both.

However, Köln have been a side where teams have been able to turn around poor form or a bad run in the season.

For example, Hamburg were without a single point on matchday four when they managed to beat the Rhine river side.

Another help for Bayer Leverkusen is Patrick Schick who has seven goals in the Bundesliga already and his form against Köln is great too with four goals and an assist in his last three games against the cathedral city side.

The form of the Czech striker has allowed Leverkusen to be a high-scoring side and they have scored 18 goals in the league which is only bettered by Bayern Munich and RB Leipzig.

Alongside this, Bayer 04 have a great record against Köln with five wins in their last five encounters in all competitions.

Ahead of the local rivalry, Bayer Leverkusen coach Carles Martínez said: “We want to give our fans a fantastic day. We want to fight for them and win.”

The away side are without Moussa Diaby, Guéla Doué, Eliesse Ben Seghir, Kennet Eichhorn, Martin Terrier and Christian Kofane.

#2533. L'avenir de Stassin ? On verra bien !

Ayant de grandes chances de faire partie des Diables Rouges convoqués demain pour aller en Turquie le 12 novembre et recevoir l'Italie 3 jours plus tard, Lucas Stassin, prêté cette saison par l'ASSE au FC Séville, s'est confié au quotidien belge Le Soir. Extraits.

"Je suis quand même resté deux saisons de suite à Saint-Etienne. Mais c’est vrai que ma dernière préparation estivale complète avec un club remonte déjà à il y a cinq ans. J’avais 16 ans et je jouais avec les U23 d’Anderlecht après avoir signé mon premier contrat pro. Depuis lors, il y a eu le transfert à Westerlo, celui à Saint-Etienne et le prêt récent à Séville sans oublier quelques grosses blessures et d’interminables négociations pour un éventuel départ de l’ASSE. On a connu mieux, question sérénité, mais j’ai appris à faire abstraction de tout cela. Je n’oublie pas que, malgré tous ces aléas, je fais le plus beau métier du monde.

Qui aurait pu dire, il y a 5 ans, que je jouerais aujourd’hui dans une équipe du Top 6 de la Liga qui se bat pour l’Europe ? Pas moi, en tout cas. J’espérais dans un coin de ma tête, mais j’avoue que j’avais des doutes. Je suis vraiment heureux à Séville, où l’entraîneur Luis Garcia me fait confiance. Je n’ai pas eu de contact personnel avec Mark van Bommel mais les kinés et les docteurs de l’équipe nationale ont pris des nouvelles de ma blessure ces derniers temps, ce qui est plutôt bon signe et encourageant. Cela veut dire que vous faites partie du projet. À peine arrivé à Séville, j’avais absolument voulu jouer contre La Corogne en dépit d’une gêne aux adducteurs. Cela m’a finalement coûté quatre à cinq semaines. C’est une bonne leçon.

Rudi Garcia m'a offert mes premières minutes avec les Diables Rouges en avril dernier, mais le Mondial m'était passé sous le nez. C’était frustrant mais, avec Saint-Etienne, j’évoluais en Ligue 2. Je n’avais donc pas grand-chose à revendiquer par rapport à mes concurrents. Désormais, je suis performant avec un club du haut du tableau en Espagne. Je pense donc avoir des raisons d’y croire. J’avais vraiment apprécié ces 18 minutes contre le Mexique, lors de la tournée aux Etats-Unis. Mais j’en garde quand même un goût de trop peu.

Je m’entraîne individuellement et je me soigne au quotidien, en me préparant moi-même des petits plats à la maison. C’est important pour la santé physique mais aussi mentale d’avoir de telles occupations en dehors des entraînements. Mon coach mental Jean-François Lenvain ne me laisse jamais tranquille avec ce genre de choses. Je dois même lui envoyer des photos de mes préparations et de mes recettes. seuls les clubs anglais semblent capables de débourser les 20 à 30 millions qu’espère Saint-Étienne pour votre transfert définitif ? J’ai décidé de jouer et de ne plus me prendre la tête avec ça. Ma priorité est d’aider Séville à décrocher un ticket européen ! Pour le reste, on verra bien.

Je ne dirais certainement pas non à un retour à Anderlecht dans une dizaine d’années. Le RSCA reste dans mon cœur. J’ai été impressionné par leurs débuts en Europa League durant les préliminaires. C’est plus compliqué depuis la fin du mercato, mais je pense que le noyau possède beaucoup plus de qualité que les dernières saisons. Je suis heureux de voir mon ami Mario Stroeykens revenir à son meilleur niveau et mon ancien équipier Léo Pétrot répondre aux attentes en défense. Léo m’avait appelé avant de signer et je l’avais aussi mis en contact avec mon papa. Nous lui avions évidemment dit tout le bien que l’on pensait du club. Je crois qu’il se plait vraiment bien en Belgique, malgré la météo.

Il y avait encore près de 25 degrés ce mardi à Séville…Et, comme par hasard, je reçois beaucoup plus de visites de mes amis ici à Séville qu’à Saint-Étienne… Si j'ai le bonheur de marquer contre l'Italie le 15 novembre ? Marquer contre l’Italie et offrir la victoire à la Belgique a toujours été l’un de mes plus grands rêves. Je l’ai toujours promis, depuis des années, à mes deux grands amis italiens que je charrie volontiers. Il s’agit des frères Belfiore. Massimo était mon entraîneur à Braine-le-Comte et Nicola a joué avec mon père."

#2532. The tale of Queen’s Park: the early innovators who became last of the amateurs

The year is 1872. Ulysses S. Grant is elected president of the US, Yellowstone becomes the world’s first national park, and the first case of Horse Flu is reported in Toronto, Canada, which will substantially disrupt life in North America by mid-December.

Meanwhile, on 30 November, the first international association football match to be retrospectively recognized by FIFA as “official” takes place at Hamilton Crescent, Glasgow, between Scotland and England. And remarkably, the whole Scottish team is comprised of players from just one single club.

If someone says the word Glasgow to you, what images does it conjure up as a football fan? I am guessing the Old Firm – Celtic vs Rangers, Green vs Blue, Catholic vs Protestant – the two historically strongest teams in Scotland involved in one of the world’s most passionate derbies. Two teams which, while sworn enemies, need each other to feed off, as seen during the recent years when Rangers’ absence from the top flight resulted in the loss of this great match-up.

What you might not be aware of, however, is that halfway between the famous theatres of Ibrox and Celtic Park, to the south, sits the 52,000-capacity Hampden Park, home of the national team, which was built in 1903 and was the biggest stadium in the world at the time.

Hampden Park was in fact the third stadium built in the area under that name. The very first was initially used in October 1873 by the oldest club in Scottish football. That club wasn’t Celtic or Rangers but a team nicknamed The Spiders, who were formed way back in 1867, making them the oldest club in the world outside of England and Wales. That club is Queen’s Park.

Queen’s Park have an important role to play in the history and development of the modern game that we know and love. In those days, they dominated Scottish football. Indeed, the early playing rules in Scotland were developed at Queen’s Park. The were also the instigators of the Scottish Football Association and the first-ever winners of the Scottish Cup in 1874.

As mentioned, 1872 saw the maiden game between Scotland and England, the former fielding 11 players all from the same team: you guessed it, Queen’s Park. The game ended 0-0 and had the great claim that the two teams used the following formations: Scotland went 2-2-6 while England fielded a more attacking 1-1-8. How the game ended 0-0 with those formations is anyone’s guess.

Highlighted by such formations, early football tactics were pretty rudimentary. It often consisted of giving the ball to one player, who then rushed upfield surrounded by teammates in a battering ram formation more similar to what we now recognise as rugby. But yet again, Queen’s Park moved the game forward. It was there that a revolutionary new tactic was invented. What if, instead of just charging with the ball until tackled, one passed the ball to a colleague when challenged?

This idea of combining together as a team, which led to the term “combination football”, helped Scottish football lead the way. Queen’s Park were the Barcelona of the 1870s, with this sharp, alien-like, movement of the ball. In March 1872, the Spiders were drawn to play against Wanderers in the FA Cup – Scottish teams took part in this famous competition until 1887 – and crushed them 5-0. A description of the game in the press included the following: “The play was now in the centre, the Queen’s Park men dribbling and passing, while their opponents indulged chiefly in heavy kicking.”

One can only imagine the stunned Englishmen watching on, monocles dropping in shock as their faces reddened beneath their handlebar moustaches. The gall of those bounders! Teamwork: what an outrageous proposition.

And so, from 1874 to 1893, Queen’s Park won ten Scottish Cups – still the third-highest total behind Celtic and Rangers – and even participated in two FA Cup finals, the only Scottish team to ever do so. They were probably the leading team across the Isles at the time, and therefore within the world.

So where did it go wrong for this club of innovators? Why did such a dominant force fade into the background? The answer to that lies in the next key development of the game: money.

When Queen’s Park were strutting their stuff and revolutionising the game, football was still a gentleman’s pastime dominated by players from public school backgrounds who played to enjoy camaraderie, healthy exercise and a few hearty drinks after. These players were usually professionals in other fields for whom football was just an amateur hobby on the side.

Early FA Cup competitions were won by teams such as Oxford University, Old Etonians and Old Carthusians – names dripping of cigar smoke, hallowed halls and back-slapping chums. But as competitions developed, the game spread into more working-class areas, especially in the north of England, until in 1983, the Old Etonians were shockingly defeated by one such outfit, Blackburn Olympic.

Long before Jack Walker and his steel money brought success to the industrial Lancashire town, Blackburn Olympic were formed. The town revolved around cotton, with mills employing thousands living a tough life fraught with danger and monotony. Football proved to be an attractive diversion and rose swiftly in popularity. However, the idea of a working-class team lifting the FA Cup was an anathema to the ex-public schoolboys that they concocted the Football Association. Surely some skullduggery must be behind such happenings.

The players of Blackburn Olympic were amateurs in that they all worked for local mills. But then again, did they truly “work” for these mills? For example, before the FA Cup final, the team had gone to Blackpool for several days of special training. But what were mill-workers doing missing several days of work? Were they still being paid during this trip? And who were these Scottish workers who played for the team? What had induced these talented footballers to leave their homeland and come south to work in a mill?

The answer, of course, was that Blackburn Olympic were arranging “jobs” for their players and supplementing their income with additional payments. These included the Scottish players, known as the “Scotch Professors”, who brought down their passing game to many of the northern teams.

And so as the northern teams began to dominate English football, tensions soon came to a head. Preston beat Upton Park in the FA Cup, who then protested against the presence of paid players in the Preston ranks. Debate raged until around 30 northern clubs threatened to break away from the FA if professionalism was not allowed. And so, in July 1885, the inevitable occurred and the professional era came into official existence.

The Scottish FA, however, were having none of it. They withdrew their teams from the FA Cup in protest. Unsurprisingly, money spoke and a significant number of Scottish players relocated south of the border, to the extent that when Preston won the first-ever Football League in 1889, they fielded ten Scottish professionals.

The Scottish Football League was launched in 1890 on an amateur basis, but Queen’s Park declined to join, on the supposition that, like in England, it would be a sham and not truly amateur. And so it proved. It didn’t take long for it to be obvious that clubs were accumulating talent through financial incentives. By 1893, Scotland too bowed to the inevitable and formalised professional football.

So what do you do if you are Queen’s Park – you are one of the innovators of the game and you believe in the amateur spirit – and see all around you paying for talent? The obvious answer is that, if you can’t beat them, join them. But this is where the story of Queen’s Park takes a remarkable twist. They decided to remain an amateur club while still playing within the Scottish League system. And they keep doing so for over 125 years.

Up until 2019, 126 years after Scotland approved professionalism, Queen’s Park stuck to their guns as an amateur outfit. Having been founded as the oldest club in Scotland, it meant they’d effectively been amateur for 152 years. Their pride in such included sporting the Latin motto Ludere Causa Ludendi (To Play for the Sake of Playing).

As the decades rolled by, maximum wages were introduced then abolished, The Bosman ruling was rendered and super-agents sent player wages into the stratosphere. Ashley Cole almost crashed his car when he heard Arsenal were only going to pay him a miserly £60,000 a week.

And still Queen’s Park held out as a final bastion of the amateur spirit, sticking to the Corinthian spirit. One almost pictures a team stuck in time, still patrolling the field in knickerbockers and finely waxed moustaches, whilst being overrun by youths in shiny, sponsored shirts. But there is a nobleness in their decision.

There was one downside to this noble stance, however: before 1893, Queen’s Park played in two FA Cup finals and won ten Scottish Cups; after 1893, they have won nothing except lower division titles.

Eventually, though, cracks appeared. In 1998, the club was stuck in the fourth tier of Scottish football. Something had to be done and so its constitution was amended to allow the signing of former professionals, providing that they still remained unpaid by Queen’s Park. Before that point in time, even ex-professionals were not permitted within Hampden Park. The manager, John McCormack, then stretched the interpretation of the new constitution to permit the loaning of professionals. And lo and behold, Queen’s Park went on to win the league and gain promotion in 2000.

Unfortunately this new approach didn’t yield increased success for long. The next season saw Queen’s Park relegated again before then finishing bottom of the last tier the following year – the first time in their history that they sat at the base of Scottish football.

The 2012/13 campaign saw Queen’s Park joined by one of their big city neighbours, Rangers, following their fall into administration. And in April 2013, Rangers hosted Queen’s Park in front of almost 50,000 fans, setting a new record for a crowd at fourth-tier level, who watched the 4-0 thumping dished out by the big boys.

Unsurprisingly, Rangers ran away with the title that year, while Queen’s Park made the playoffs but lost out to Peterhead. One member of that team was a young Andy Robertson, who wisely decided on accepting an offer to play for the Spiders rather than starting university, a decision that proved a wise choice in light of his subsequent career trajectory.

Up to this point, Queen’s Park still played their home games at the famous Hampden Park. The craziness of that situation was epitomised by Hampden having a capacity of almost 52,000 post-Taylor Report, while Queen’s Park’s average attendance was around 600-700 souls. One can only imagine the atmosphere within the vast cavernous ground as fewer than a thousand people packed together in a small group to cheer on their beloved club.

But Queen’s Park still owned the famous ground, with a lease granted to the SFA expiring 2020. In 2018, a deal was agreed by which the SFA would purchase Hampden Park outright from Queen’s Park on expiry of the lease for £5m. Queen’s Park used some of those proceeds to develop their neighbouring training ground, known cutely as Lesser Hampden, into a 1,774-capacity stadium for home games. Having played their last game at Hampden in March 2021, they have ground-shared with Falkirk, Patrick Thistle and Stenhousemuir as they continue to develop Lesser Hampden.

With plans in place to downsize their stadium, and the threat of being overtaken by teams from the Highland and Lowland Leagues, the Spiders realised that their long-held model of amateurism was no longer feasible. And so in 2019, after 152 years of proud amateur status, Queen’s Park decided to vote on whether to become professional. If 75% percent of members voted in favour, they would finally be able to hire players on full-time contracts, fend off offers from competing teams, and receive compensation for players moving on.

The realisation that they were developing youth players who then just moved elsewhere upon turning 18 was becoming too much to bear. The motion was presented on 14 November 2019 and saw 91 percent of the members vote in favour. The last bastion of amateur football was felled.

Emotions were understandably mixed. “The committee of the club plotted a course to try to defend ourselves against the threats of other ambitious clubs around us wishing to become a senior league club,” former Queen’s Park striker Crawley told BBC Scotland. “We are keen to arm ourselves to fend that off and progress. It was head against heart. The heart of a lot of the membership would be we really don’t want to do this, but we have to do it.”

The club’s official Twitter site thanked fans for their involvement, stating, “We recognise the difficulty in introducing this resolution. However, we believe this will enable us to plan for a bright future as we face up to the challenges of moving from the national stadium and maintaining our senior league place.”

Was the move successful? Well, the 2019/20 season was ended early by the COVID-19 pandemic but the summer saw Queen’s Park use their new status to sign several professional players. And lo and behold, 2020/21 saw Queen’s Park promoted to the third tier as champions, a full 16 points ahead of second place Edinburgh City. And if that wasn’t enough, the 2021/22 season saw Queen’s Park promoted yet again through the Championship playoffs, defeating Airdrieonians over two legs.

As of today, Queen’s Park now sit atop the Scottish Championship, promotion from which would take them into the Premiership and back with the likes of their lofty neighbours, Celtic and Rangers.

There is a part of me that is sad to think that, in order to survive, Queen’s Park had to finally bow to modern pressures and lose their status as the only amateurs still standing. But despite this change, Queen’s Park still have an amazing legacy that they can proudly cling to.

For their first five years of existence, they never conceded a goal. For their first seven years of existence, they never lost a game. They won the Scottish Cup ten times and even participated in two FA Cup finals. They are even credited with being the first team to play two halves of 45 minutes each with the novel concept of a half-time break, as well as introducing free-kicks, turnstiles and crossbars. Imagine a game of football without crossbars.

The Spiders came up with the heretic notion of passing the ball rather than just rushing it upfield – a notion which some teams still struggle with even today. They organised the first Scotland vs England game and fielded the entire Scottish team. And then they maintained their amateur ethos despite their last trophy of significance being the Scottish Cup in 1893.

Somewhere, a former Queen’s Park director is looking down from above. “Damn fine to see our boys getting back to where they belong. But playing for money, well that is just utter rot. Man should stretch his sinews for the pure love of the game itself.” And with that, he puffs on his oversized cigar, downs his 15-year-old single malt, and twiddles his beautifully waxed moustache.

#2531. The madness of Liverpool’s first foray into European competition

Six-time European Cup winners; three-time UEFA Cup winners; a club synonymous with continental glory and famous nights under the floodlights: when it comes to European competition and experiences, you can safely say that Liverpool have been there, done that and bought many t-shirts. Few teams have as much history across the continent. Crazy nights in Istanbul, thundering roars against St-Étienne, and the despair of Heysel.

But every voyage begins with a first tentative step. And for Liverpool, that initial journey into the great unknown commenced in the summer of 1964. The nation was gripped by Beatlemania as the Fab Four started to conquer the world. Such was the fervour, cinemas saw the release of A Hard Day’s Night accompanied by screaming teenagers while The Beatles’ return to their home city saw 300 people injured as around 150,000 attempted to see their idols in person. Liverpool became the epicentre of Britain, relegating London to a minor role – at least until the Swinging Sixties exploded into full flowering.

And while Liverpool dominated the pop scene, it also dominated the football world. Bill Shankly led Liverpool to the Second Division title in 1962, bringing the Reds back into the limelight. Incredibly, just two seasons later, Liverpool were English champions for the first time since 1947, powered by the goals of Roger Hunt, Ian St John and Alf Arrowsmith, who combined for 92 strikes that season.

That Championship was rewarded by Liverpool’s maiden entry onto the European stage – a place in the European Cup against the elite of the continent. It was a competition that, at the time, was still purely contested between league champions, a straight knockout from the beginning. No groups, no seeding.

It is almost impossible now to imagine Liverpool looking ahead to European competition as an unknown quantity, but it must be remembered that, until Shankly won that title in 1964, Liverpool had been in the doldrums.

Early British escapades into Europe were led by the likes of Wolves, Manchester United, Dundee and Hibs. As those clubs visited Barcelona, Madrid and Milan, Liverpool were mired in the Second Division, travelling to the slightly less glamourous metropolises of Scunthorpe, Plymouth and Rotherham. But all that had changed over the course of three seasons, and Liverpool were now playing alongside the giants of Europe.

The competition began with a preliminary round, although in effect it was basically a full round with only the previous season’s winners, Internazionale, afforded a bye. Given the straight draw and lack of seeding, Liverpool could theoretically have drawn anyone, including the likes of Real Madrid or Benfica – a potential recipe for a short-lived adventure.

But they managed to avoid the big boys and instead started their first European expedition with a trip to Iceland, whose champions were KR.

That season, KR became the first Icelandic team to enter the European Cup, so a trip to their island was uncommon in those days. And what a trip it was. Now there are probably direct flights from Liverpool to Iceland, but in those days it was somewhat more convoluted. Leaving from Liverpool, the team first had to travel to Manchester, then fly from Manchester to London, then fly from London to Prestwick, and finally from there on to Reykjavik.

What is more admirable is that some Liverpool supporters actually made the trip themselves. For many, with Liverpool having never been in Europe, this could have been their first trip outside of England. As one fan put it at the time, “We thought they were all eskimos,” before being surprised to find they were just like scousers. Different times, indeed.

Ayrshire was also the home county of Shankly, so he couldn’t resist showing off the area to his boys before travelling on to Iceland. Full of pride, Shanks hired a bus to take the team to see … Butlin’s holiday camp. On proudly telling the camp leader that they were Liverpool FC, en route to Iceland, the gentleman helpfully told them, “I think you’ve taken the wrong road, sir.”

And so, on 17 August 1964, Liverpool strode out for the very first time in European competition on the green grass of Reyjavik before 10,000 witnesses. Opening night nerves and a lack of experience affected them; at half-time, Liverpool were carrying a slim 1-0 advantage heading into the break. But then fitness levels and experience kicked in and two goals from Wallace and Hunt saw them run out 5-0 winners.

A month later, the Icelanders made the long trip over to Anfield and, sadly for them, took another pasting, this time losing 6-1 with Ian St John grabbing a double. The competition was already down to just 16 teams and the draw for the first round proper awaited.

This time, Liverpool would not be getting such a straightforward tie. Fate threw them up against the Belgian champions, Anderlecht. This was an side that would then go on to win the following four Belgian First Division titles too – a streak that is still a national record.

It was a team led by the legendary Paul Van Himst, the scorer of 233 goals in 457 appearances for the club 16 seasons. This team was so good that when Belgium played against the Netherlands in 1964, there were ten Anderlecht players within the starting line-up. The only non-Anderlecht starter was the goalkeeper, Standard Leige’s Guy Delhasse, but he was substituted during the game by Jean-Marie Trappeniers who was, of course, an Anderlecht player. Liverpool had their work cut out.

Ahead of the game, Shankly decided on a rebranding of his team. Liverpool had traditionally played in red shirts and white shorts, but the manager decided that playing in all red would make the players look bigger and more intimidating – and so was born the legendary kit that we know and love so well today. Shankly was also aware of the threat posed by Van Himst and decided to throw a tough young defender up against him by the name of Tommy Smith.

And so onto Anfield for the first leg on 25 November. As the players were getting set to leave the changing room, Shankly gave them his final words of advice: “Right boys. You’ve nothing to beat tonight, they’re a load of rubbish.”

Ninety minutes later, Liverpool had swept the Belgians aside 3-0, with goals from St John, Hunt and Ron Yeats. Shankly’s words on the Reds re-entering the changing room were plain: “Congratulations, you’ve just beaten one of the best teams in Europe.”

A further 1-0 win in Brussels saw Liverpool through to the quarter-finals with four wins out of four under their belt. They seemed to be getting the hang of this European thing. But now some serious weeding out had been done and the competition was down to the major players, including the ever-dangerous trio of Real Madrid, Benfica and Inter. The hope would be to avoid one of those in the next round.

As luck would have it, Real Madrid drew Benfica, meaning one of those powers would be eliminated at this stage. Inter drew Scottish champions Rangers leaving Liverpool to face German champions Köln. It wasn’t the hardest tie but was far from the easiest either.

Köln had gained entry into the European Cup by becoming winners of the first-ever Bundesliga in 1963. A 0-0 stalemate in Germany was followed by the same back on Merseyside; 180 minutes of football and no goals to show for it. Back then, the next step was a playoff at a neutral venue.

And so, on 25 March 1965, Liverpool and Köln fans met up at Feyenoord’s De Kuip for the decider. The Reds got off to a cracking start, with St. John tapping in from close range on 20 minutes followed by a Roger Hunt second on 37 minutes. Obviously the all-red strip was having the desired effect.

But Köln got back into the match through a Karl-Heinz Thielen header a minute later, meaning the Reds went into half-time 2-1 up. Shortly after kick-off, the Germans scored again, this time through Hannes Löhr, to level matters – and that is how the game remained until the final whistle.

So how do you decide a high-profile European Cup quarter-final when the playoff is tied? At the time, penalties were not in use, and so it came down to the ultimate test of football skills and composure – the coin toss. Unbelievable to think now but yes, a critical European Cup game would be decided on the flip of a coin. In the middle of the pitch. Televised.

Up stepped the captains, Ron Yeats and Hans Sturm. Under the floodlights, Yeats recalls the events wonderfully: “I thought, get in quick, I’ll have tails referee. And before he asked, he went yeah – Liverpool tails, Köln heads. Up it went and it come down and I couldn’t believe it, it stuck in a divot on its side. We were trying to blow over to tails by now and of course the German captain was trying to blow over to heads. And he had more puff than I had. It was coming over to heads and I said to the referee, ref, you’re going to have to re-toss the coin. And he said you’re right Mr Yeats and he picked it up. I thought, great, up it went again.”

Over 48,000 fans held their breath and waited for some sort of signal – and then the Liverpool players were jumping for joy. The Reds had just progressed to the semi-finals of the European Cup thanks to Yeats and tails.

Shankly went over to the Köln bench to commiserate, saying that it was no way to decide such a hard-fought, evenly-balanced game. And so, in their maiden continental odyssey, Liverpool found themselves in the European Cup semi-finals.

Joining them would be holders Inter, two-time champions Benfica, and ETO Győr, a Hungarian side who had just won the league for the first time. Probably hoping for Győr, Liverpool were instead drawn against Inter. If the Reds were hoping to get to the final in, appropriately enough, Milan, they were going to have to overcome Helenio Herrera’s men.

Just how daunting a task would that be? Well, Il Grande Inter, as they would later be named in recognition of their prowess, had lifted the European Cup the year before, defeating the dominant Real Madrid 3-1. They were managed by perhaps the world’s most revered manager, Herrera, who had added steel through the adoption of a fifth defender, the libero, to elevate catenaccio to an art form.

The team oozed quality: Sarti, Burgnich, Facchetti, Picchi, Corso, Mazzola, Suárez and Jair. They were on their way to recapturing the Scudetto, having just thrashed AC Milan 5-2. This definitely wasn’t Scunthorpe anymore.

Before that trip, however, Liverpool did have the small matter of an FA Cup final to contest against Don Revie’s Leeds. The crown jewel of the English season saw Liverpool lift the trophy after a 2-1 win, with all the goals in extra time. Wild celebrations followed back on Merseyside as the trophy was paraded before an estimated 250,000 people singing Beatles’ songs. For the first time in their history, the trophy sat within the halls of Anfield.

Just three days later, Liverpool welcomed Inter with players still suffering from an extra-time final on the spacious Wembley pitch and some lingering hangovers. Anticipation was at fever pitch with the ground full at 17.30 for an evening kick-off. Officially, 54,000 fans packed out Anfield, but there were probably more squeezed in.

If that wasn’t intimidating enough for Inter, Shankly then cranked up his mind games again. Wouldn’t it be a great idea if the FA Cup was paraded once again around the ground just before kick-off? And so it was, pitching the Liverpool fans into a state of pure rapture and upping the volume considerably. It was time for Liverpool to really see how they matched up against the great Inter.

In a later interview, Ian St. John describes the game as “the night of nights”. Although at this point no-one knew how many great European Anfield nights the future would hold, this one would still go down alongside the best.

After just three minutes, a Callaghan cross was beautifully converted by Hunt. Mazzola then struck back shortly after for Inter, but after that it was all Liverpool. After 33 minutes, a lovely free-kick routine saw Callaghan dummy taking it, then run on as the free-kick was taken to get it back into his stride before hammering it into the corner. It was straight off the training ground. By now Anfield was a thundering cauldron of noise as Liverpool kept sweeping forward. A shell-shocked Inter staggered into the dressing room at half-time to attempt to catch their breath.

After 74 minutes, a shot by Hunt was parried by Sarti straight into the path of St. John, who gratefully accepted the tap-in. It was job done on Merseyside. Liverpool had a two-goal advantage to take to Milan, meaning that the kings of catenaccio would have to come out of their shells. To this day, many older Liverpool fans will still swear that this Anfield night was the team’s greatest display and the club’s most atmospheric evening.

Ominously, the Italian press warned Liverpool that “you will never be allowed to win”. The shenanigans began as soon as the squad arrived at their Lake Como base. Church bells and singing locals ensured that sleep would be a luxury. And that was before the Reds had to step out in front of 76,000 at the San Siro. The game was going to need a firm and brave referee – and the man chosen for this big night was one José María Ortiz de Mendíbil from Spain.

After eight minutes, Inter were awarded a free-kick just outside the area, which the Liverpool players believed was signalled as indirect by Ortiz. The referee strode out the ten yards for the wall, using the most exaggerated, extended strides you will ever see, before pushing the Liverpool wall back to what is more like 20 yards from the free-kick. Corso then struck a lovely shot into the corner – directly. A goal was awarded and Inter had just the start they needed.

Just two minutes later, things went from bad to worse. Inter forward Joaquín Peiró chased a long ball but Liverpool goalkeeper Tommy Lawrence was paying attention and got to it first. Bouncing the ball as he moved up the area, Lawrence was unaware of Peiró coming up behind him, who stole the ball on a bounce and rolled it into the empty net.

The move represented what was generally viewed as “ungentlemanly conduct”, along with Peiró coming from an offside position, although I have to confess that it looked a fair enough steal to me. The Liverpool players protested vehemently but to no avail: Ortiz let it stand. Liverpool’s two-goal advantage was wiped out in the space of ten mad minutes.

A third Inter goal by the glorious Facchetti after 62 minutes saw the Liverpool dream finally extinguished, even though St. John had a goal disallowed for an infringement unknown to him. The game ended 3-0 and Inter went on to reclaim the trophy in their own stadium against Benfica. The last image from that night in the San Siro was Yeats chasing Ortiz, calling him “El Bastardo” while kicking him on the ankles. It was a sad end to a glorious run.

Interestingly enough, the secretary of Inter at the time was Italo Allodi, who was later accused of match-fixing scandals including the infamous Juventus-Derby game in 1973. Allodi was close to a Hungarian called Dezső Solti who was accused by a referee in 1966 of trying to bribe him before the 1964 European Cup final with “enough dollars to buy five Mercedes”.

Later, UEFA and journalistic investigations found Solti guilty of offering bribes between 1963 and 1967. None of this proves that anything deliberately untoward was manifest that night, but suspicion lingers.

If skullduggery was indeed at work that night, the football gods made sure to rectify the situation going forwards. While Shankly would never win the European Cup, Bob Paisley, Joe Fagan, Rafa Benitez and Jurgen Klopp would see that Liverpool lifted ol’ big ears six times, while Inter would only enjoy one more night of glory in 2010.

The 1964/65 European Cup campaign became the springboard for Liverpool’s dominance on the continent. They played in Europe for an incredible 20 successive seasons after their inaugural visit and still remain one of the most successful clubs in history. And it all started with a campaign that included visiting little-known Iceland, winning games through a twice-taken coin toss, and one of the greatest nights that Anfield has ever witnessed, before being curtailed in somewhat dubious circumstances. It taught Liverpool many lessons on the wily ways of European competition – lessons that they learned well and applied with a vengeance going forwards.

#2529. Access, Not Chances: How Como Took Manchester United Apart – LC

von Next Generation am 24.10.2026 in den Kategorien Champions League,Uncategorized mit 0 Kommentaren

Como beat Manchester United 4-1 — and in this case the scoreline really is a fair expression of the game. For long stretches the match was one-sided, not in terms of chance volume but in terms of access: Como dictated in every phase where the ball went, who received it, and under what conditions it had to be played on. United never got on terms in any of those phases.

Cesc Fàbregas’s side showed two things that rarely go together. Out of possession, a pressing structure with clear direction, isolation of one side, and man-marking that applied near the ball only for the action in progress while everything around it was already speculating on the second ball. And in possession, a positional game of remarkable technical maturity that ties opponents in, switches play, and works systematically into the space behind them. The 4-1 is therefore less an outlier than evidence of work that has been built up over recent seasons.

This analysis goes through the phases one by one: first Como in possession and United’s response, then the work against the ball from the high press to defending the box, and finally the goals.

In build-up, Como moved away from their nominal 4-2-3-1 and sat closer to a 4-3-3, with the eights as the real variable: very free in their movement and above all active in the half-spaces. The base structure stayed stable — a flat four plus a six in front of them, sometimes ahead of the line, sometimes slightly behind the forward line.

Close to their own goal, Como consistently involved goalkeeper Butez as an additional central player in the build-up. That created a five-against-four against United’s front line — awkward on paper, more awkward still in execution, because Como didn’t use the overload to play through but to tie opponents in. From there, the same shape kept appearing on both the right and the left: centre-back, full-back, a player from the half-position in the eight space, and a six alongside. A diamond that wasn’t an end in itself but had a purpose: the diagonal route out.

Como’s build-up structure. From a 4-1 (flat four), the half-positions with Paz and Perrone are added. Small triangles and diamonds emerge. The three attackers ahead of them create additional options in behind.

One detail deserves particular attention. At times the full-backs stood not wide but flat, in a half-position. That sounds like a nuance, but it had a clear function: it vacated the wing and created the space the eights could drop into — Paz and Perrone did so regularly. If you don’t occupy the wing yourself, you force the opponent to guard it anyway.

ManU got no access near the ball and left large diagonal spaces open on the far side.

For United’s midfielders this was close to unsolvable. Tielemans, Mount and Andrey Santos spent the whole game struggling to establish any responsibility at all, let alone get into duels. When an eight dropped out, they couldn’t follow, and if one of them was tied in by it, a two-against-one opened up on the far wing. Como didn’t create that situation once; they created it serially.

The decisive mechanism lay in receiving and relaying the ball. The player in the deeper position usually worked with two touches: the first took the ball backwards, the second relayed it to the free teammate. At the end of every one of those actions stood a player with the game in front of him — and from that open position he could switch play.

What made it possible were the preparatory movements. Paz and captain Da Cunha first lured their markers in the wrong direction and then worked with the head start they’d bought. That head start isn’t only space, it’s above all time: time to control the ball first and relay it second, rather than having to do both in one touch.

A scene in the 17th minute is typical. Mason Mount steps out, Mazraoui goes into the duel at the same time — nominally United are in an overload here. Only neither of them really gets to the opponent, because both are too late. Paz can therefore calmly take his touch backwards and switch play directly with the second, dissolving the pressure before it had even formed.

When the build-up ran down the right, it showed in a recurring pattern: Paz and Da Cunha took the first touch backwards or inside and then switched with the left foot out of a closed body position. The players they found were systematically in open positions — Milla, the far-side six, or the wing-to-wing connection directly.

In the second half, Como adjusted the shape and more often played with two sixes, to form the diamonds faster and switch to the far-side six faster. Couto stood out in this: the right-back repeatedly played the chip into the six or ten space — six or seven times in a row during one spell. It was mainly Paz who received the ball there and could launch the attack.

In the second half Como focus on those diagonal spaces and look for them mainly with chipped balls.

Especially striking inside the diamond are the rotations between the players. Because of the connections between them, they moved in rotation depending on running path, space and ball movement. The same recurring spaces were re-occupied. Under pressure on the ball carrier, the outer players pushed flat or ran in behind the opponents; the highest player in the diamond burst out of the cover shadow and became the reference point in depth. On a deep pass, the two wide players released and joined the depth, able to offer themselves again immediately in an open body position. If the pass went via the flat wide players, the spaces in front of the new ball carrier could be run into. Every rotation, every positional adjustment was coordinated with the others and, in the end, produced a stream of pressure-resolving passes while Manchester United chased the press in vain.

Como bridged the middle third exceptionally quickly, via dribbles at speed and diagonal passes. Behind it stood a recognisable principle: first tie the opponent to one wing, then get behind them via the diagonal, and in the final third do the same thing again. In sum, that produced the shape of a Z — build-up on the right, transition on the left, finish from the right again.

In this example Como pin ManU in the press with their short passing. Thanks to the extra attacker on the ball-near side they can play through the pressure and switch.

The connections between wingers and striker carried these attacks and were deliberately kept flexible. The wide players sometimes stayed wide, sometimes came short, sometimes tucked inside. Douvikas accordingly either waited deep in front of the line for the chipped ball or, when space opened up on the wing, received the deeper pass and then ran in behind along the wing as soon as the ball sat on the full-back’s open foot. That flexibility produced the overloads on one side, and the overloads produced the central overload.

In the final third one element was added that is rare in fast transition teams: patience. Como drew opponents towards them with dribbles and short passes, used back-passes when needed, and then switched in front of the block. Da Cunha, Baturina and Paz were the three who could tie opponents in and then find the diagonal. They created the positions on the diagonal, were available to receive, and used their space to apply pressure. The block was usually tied in with short touches, the switch and the position behind the opponent prepared, and quick finishes sought.

The intensity in possession shouldn’t be underestimated. Practically every attack involved at least three or four players, the width was occupied, the centre likewise, and there were multiple options throughout. What they looked for almost every time was the space behind the opponent on the far side — exactly the zone that is closed last when a block shifts across.

Paz brought the right profile for this pattern. A strong left foot cutting inside from the right, he repeatedly dropped into the right-hand blind side of his opponents, got shots away there with few touches, and racked up the most shots of the match. That the finishes generally came off few touches isn’t a detail but the consequence of the time won further back.

As the move continues, Como again look for the blind side of the defenders. Here Douvikas and Paz are positioned in behind, with Da Cunha joining in front of the back line. After briefly pinning the opponents, the switch reaches Da Cunha, who misses the target from distance.

What carries all of this is the technical execution. Soft passes, sharp ones when the moment called for it, consistently well timed and played into the right foot — for ninety minutes. Passing that secure at that tempo is what makes a variable concept actually retrievable.

United’s basic problem out of possession was the hybrid. They pressed half man-oriented, but with a plus-one in the last line — a construction that forces compromises in both directions. It demands access up front that it doesn’t fund at the back.

There were certainly moments when it worked. When Dalot pushed through into the press on the right and passed his man backwards, a clean man-orientation emerged all the way up to the front — and with it a clear assignment for Mount, out of which the ball could be won. But that stayed the exception. Most of the time Dalot sat too flat out of possession, so United couldn’t trigger the duels up front and went into the press a man short.

Mount was the most conspicuous case in this respect. He barely got into a duel across the whole match because the decision to step out always came too late. Against a team that works with preparatory movements, a late decision is practically the same as none: the opponent already has the head start before the journey even begins.

On top of that came a problem on the far side. There, United’s winger frequently failed to protect the depth — usually Mbeumo, who jogged back rather than closing the space. But that was precisely the target zone of Como’s diagonal switches. It allowed them to change sides almost at will and regularly find an open structure on the other side.

The second half brought no improvement. The distances to opponents stayed large, and centrally what was actually intended still didn’t materialise. Michael Carrick’s double change at half-time was meant to bring more physicality to the six with Carlos Baleba, but it didn’t solve the problem — the assignments still didn’t work, the duels in the centre still didn’t happen, and the wide positions dragged the eights out further, which opened new spaces.

In the end the energy simply wasn’t there to press with the necessary intensity. United’s press became increasingly static, and a static press against an opponent with this passing quality yields not a single ball win — it only hands them the starting positions they’re looking for anyway.

Como started the game in a high press and sustained it above all in the first half, with isolated spells after the break too. It was triggered on back-passes and goal kicks — and, remarkably, by their own goals: after every strike the team immediately pushed high again instead of managing the lead in a deeper block.

The steering ran consistently towards United’s left build-up side. The reason is personnel: Harry Maguire played there with his weaker left foot as the outside foot, and next to him stood another right-footer in Noussair Mazraoui on the left. Two right-footers on one build-up side means the ball always has to be taken back inside to play on — exactly where Como were closing the door. The press was usually triggered by the left-sided forward, with the ball ending up at either Maguire or Mazraoui.

Pressing structure after the change in the second half.

In the centre Como defended man-oriented, with both sixes pushing very high and taking flat passes into United’s midfielders out of the game. That removed the short route through the middle as an option and left the long ball. But the long ball triggered nothing for United: the second balls went to Como practically every time.

That isn’t chance, it’s running work. The sixes consistently tracked the negative pressing paths back, anticipated the long ball early, and were in front of the line before it arrived. Luis Milla covered the most ground of any player on the pitch in this pattern — recovery runs after being played past, doubling up on the ball, attacking the second ball. When the long ball came from Maguire or one of the full-backs, a six was regularly in front of the line with an intense sprint, sealing the centre, taking away the diagonal follow-up passes and attacking the loose balls.

Defending the long ball and extra support on the second ball.

Once the ball was on one side, that side was isolated. The near-side forward had access to the centre-back and dropped back a touch when the pass went to the full-back, so that he covered centre-back and six simultaneously and closed the square pass. The far-side forward marked the goalkeeper. That served two functions at once: it took away United’s escape route via the keeper along with the switch, and it kept a player in position for the moment of the turnover.

The double pivot worked in a deliberate half-position. The near-side six stayed tight to his man, the far-side one dropped slightly behind him — securing the space and at the same time establishing access to the far-side centre-back in United’s back three.

The last line defended largely man-for-man in the high press, and very tightly. But the decisive detail is the one that carries the whole concept: the man-marking near the ball applied only to the action in progress. Around it, players were already releasing from their assignments and orienting towards the second ball. That is only retrievable at very high intensity — and it’s the reason United’s forced long balls produced not a single usable moment.

When Como dropped a line, the man-orientation at the second height stayed exceptionally high. Here too the back-pass served as the trigger to push up as a unit and tip back into man-marking. If United were in a closed body position, the Comaschi had already pushed up far enough to attack the back-pass at the moment it was played — a pattern that led directly to the 3-0. The half-position was at work there again: closing down level with the centre-back while simultaneously prepared to push through onto the next player deeper.

In the first half Como still had problems assigning the half-positions against ManU’s 3-2-5. In this example Fernandes moves to the wing, Como fail to establish responsibility and access and are played through at mid-height.

Up to half-time, though, Como had a structural problem with this. The pure man-marking didn’t come off cleanly, because of the two sixes in front of the line, one should really have tucked in and taken over the half-position for the centre-backs. That didn’t happen consistently. The consequence: a centre-back stepped out, the space behind him stayed unoccupied, and that was exactly what United kept attacking.

The pattern of pressing onto Manchester United’s left side continued in the second half, now with Dalot at full-back. The press was still triggered by Douvikas, with the ball-near winger pushing up as a second striker to press the centre-back. Paz focused on the visitors’ ball-near six and Couto pushed through onto Dalot. Through the central connections the game was isolated on one side.

At half-time Como adjusted. One six moved directly into the line, with the far-side winger pushing in onto the six — they now pressed out of a 5-2-1-2. Lucas Da Cunha pressed through from the right onto Maguire, while Nico Paz picked up United’s six. The central forward pressed the central centre-back and from there carried on to the goalkeeper. The far-side wide midfielder and the remaining six were poised — one onto United’s free six, one onto the far-side centre-back — again in that half-position between opponent and the space to be protected.

ManU building up high against the man-oriented Italians.

The effect was immediately visible. Como controlled the half-positions, got access right into the last line throughout, and set the rhythm of the match from then on. The adjustment solved exactly the problem the first half had left open, without changing the underlying principle.

In the final phase Como defended from a very deep line inside the box. That left barely any space behind the line to play into — which United hardly looked for anyway. The visitors attacked little depth and rarely occupied the width in the final third, so Como could stay compact without being punished for it.

Accordingly, a lot of players stood very centrally in and in front of the box. That produced numerous cover shadows and a numerical superiority around the ball, which allowed them to steer actively within the block rather than merely react. At times they deliberately steered inside, to guide the opponent into the prepared congestion and cover from a deeper position.

Deep back line in the box, centre closed and play steered inside. The box defending allowed the English side shots from distance.

The crucial point is that Como left the gaps open not despite this set-up but because of it. If you pack the box and secure the depth, you give up the space in front of the line — and that is exactly where United were supposed to shoot from.

The numbers vindicate the decision. Taking the conceded goal out of the box, Como allowed nine shots from outside the penalty area across ninety minutes, worth roughly 0.24 xG combined; only three of them were even on target. On top of that, of the four shots inside the box, two came from at least 14 to 15 metres and effectively belong in the long-range category as well. United accepted the invitation, and the shooting quality was perfectly decent — it’s just that with that scoring probability over a whole match, the most likely outcome is precisely what happened.

1-0, Nico Paz (2′) — The goal comes before United are even set. Como take the goal kick quickly and play straight to the full-back, luring the visitors into the press — only not cleanly. Mason Mount triggers the pressure and is played past, and on the second ball he goes into the duel as well instead of holding the middle and looking for the space where he could pick the ball up. That is exactly what opens the centre, the space for the second ball is there, and Paz can play it through.

What follows is the transition principle in its purest form: a quick switch to the diagonal side, then short passes against a very deep line. Matheus Cunha doesn’t pick up the player behind him and leaves the ten space open, where Paz is standing. He needs two touches because neither Cunha from above nor Maguire from his distance gets into the duel. Lammens, for his part, doesn’t defend the goal with his cover shadow but takes the near post — which leaves the far corner to a strong left foot. The curled finish into it is quality, though.

2-0, Anastasios Douvikas (49′, assist Paz) — Same zone, same cause. Bryan Mbeumo doesn’t choose to shut the centre but closes the centre-back, which creates the space for Da Cunha, who drives forward and plays Martin Baturina in. Centrally, Mount again fails to pick up his man. Out wide, Amad Diallo, introduced at half-time, generates no pressure centrally, so Mazraoui can’t take over the deep wide player in the first place.

The result is Paz behind the opponent, in the ten space, dribbling unopposed at goal. Matthijs de Ligt establishes no reference to Douvikas’s run in behind and slips as well — a one-against-one with the goalkeeper that Douvikas finishes coolly.

3-0, Nico Paz (57′) — The only goal that comes directly out of the work against the ball. Como are in the midfield press in a closed position with access to the inside lane, win the ball there and punish a lapse of concentration in the visitors’ build-up. At the end, Paz is alone in front of Lammens again.

4-0, Jacobo Ramón (76′, assist Kaiki) — This goal comes from a longer possession spell and shows the assignment problem in its clearest form. Mazraoui is too narrow and has no access to the wide player. Diallo stands in the space with access to neither the central player nor the full-back nor the winger — an orientation that doesn’t work in any direction.

Como switch to Máximo Perrone, who can slide the ball out wide because the space is simply free. From there comes the cross from an open body position, and in the centre Como run a two-against-one at the end of which Ramón scores.

4-1, Joshua Zirkzee (90′) — Como’s only lapse, and one in a phase in which the team had already dropped very deep. United get the ball to the wing, it’s two against two in the box, and the cross is initially only feinted. Zirkzee orients himself well forwards at that moment, while Como’s defenders are already stepping out after the feinted cross. Diallo’s ball in behind the line is strong, the offside call is razor-thin in his favour, and Zirkzee takes two touches before finishing one-against-one with the goalkeeper. A blemish, not a structural problem.

The common denominator — Three of Como’s four goals come in the same zone: the space in front of and behind United’s line that nobody takes responsibility for. Mount is involved in two of those scenes through an assignment he never establishes, Diallo in two more after coming on. Nico Paz is at the heart of three of the four goals, twice as scorer, once as the provider — always out of the opponent’s blind side.

Como’s 4-1 has one common cause across every phase, and it lies less in any single idea than in its execution under continuous load. Out of possession they steered, isolated and speculated on the second ball — with man-marking that applied near the ball only for the action in progress and was already dissolved around it. In possession they tied opponents in, switched play and worked systematically into the space behind. Both require eleven players to anticipate a teammate’s decision, and both functioned over the full distance.

The connecting detail is the half-position. In the press, the far-side six stood between opponent and the space to be protected; in possession, the eights worked out of the same zone, and the full-backs occupied it flat in order to vacate the wing for exactly those players. Como, in other words, occupied the position in both directions from which two jobs can be done at once — which is why the side could neither be played past in the press nor be pinned in the build-up.

For United the diagnosis is more uncomfortable than the result. The problem wasn’t individual errors but that the team never, in any phase, got into the duels it needed. The mixture of half man-orientation and a plus-one in the last line funded no access up front, and without access every decision is made too late — from Mount in the centre to Mbeumo failing to secure the far-side depth. Carrick’s double change at half-time was meant to bring physicality to the six with Carlos Baleba, but it changed nothing about the assignments, and in Diallo a player came on wide who reached neither one opponent nor the other in his space. That the energy ran out in the end was a consequence rather than a cause.

That leaves the context. A 4-1 against Manchester United is no proof in itself, and Como’s finishing was exceptionally efficient on the night. What showed regardless of the result, though, is a team with a recognisable, rehearsed answer in all four phases of the game — and one that put a technical maturity on the pitch in possession that you wouldn’t readily credit to a side promoted two years ago.
